Does this predicate apply A→B or B→A? (Answer "A→B" or "B→A")
Use the OSM coordinates, bounding boxes, and hierarchy below to reason about the spatial relation. Do not guess.

Vernacular-to-Topology Reference:
  WITHIN    — "is in", "located in", "part of"              (A is fully inside B)
  CONTAINS  — "is home to", "includes", "hosts"             (A fully encloses B)
  TOUCHES   — "borders", "adjacent to", "across from" (water boundary) (partial), "enclave of" (shared boundary, not interior)
  CROSSES   — "passes through", "along", "traverses",
              "at the mouth of" / "upstream from" (full LineString)
  DISJOINT  — "far from", "miles away", "separate from" (large distance)
  OVERLAPS  — "partly in", "extends into", "straddles",
              "part of population in", "county seat of" (partial area overlap)
  EQUALS    — "identical perimeter", "same boundary as", "mirrors exactly",
              "exact same extent as", "perfect mapping symmetry"
              (A and B share the exact same geometry: same interior, boundary, exterior)

--- 4-STEP DISAMBIGUATION (run before answering within, touches, or overlaps) ---
Step 1: Do A and B have the EXACT SAME geometry (identical extent)? -> equals (not within/contains)
Step 2: Is A completely inside B? -> within (not overlaps)
Step 3: Is B completely inside A? -> contains (not overlaps)
Step 4: Do A and B share SOME area but NEITHER fully contains the other? -> overlaps
Strong overlaps signals: "partly in", "extends into", "straddles",
                           "part of population in", "county seat of" (city crosses county line).
Strong equals signals: "identical perimeter", "same boundary", "municipal limits mirror county lines",
                         "perfect mapping symmetry", "coextensive".

  [ToT] ── BRANCHES_RAW ──
Does "Columbus,wisconsin on crawfish river,wisconsin" apply A→B or B→A? (Answer with one direction)
--- Branch 1 ---
Reasoning (use OSM data, not just bbox). Cite coordinates, bbox overlap, and hierarchy clues.
Strong equals signals: "identical perimeter", "same boundary", etc. (Do not use equals unless geometry is identical)
If the text is ambiguous, explore THREE different predicates (contains, within, touches, crosses, disjoint, overlaps).
Each branch must pick EXACTLY ONE predicate from: contains, within, touches, crosses, disjoint, overlaps, equals
End with: Answer: [predicate]"

We are an expert in geospatial topological reasoning.

Vernacular-to-Topology Reference:
  WITHIN    — "is in", "located in", "part of"              (A is fully inside B)
  CONTAINS  — "home to", "includes", "hosts"               (A encloses B)
  TOUCHES   — "borders", "adjacent to", "across from" (water) (share boundary, no interior overlap)
  CROSSES   — "passes through", "along", "at the mouth of" (full LineString through Polygon)
  DISJOINT  — "far from", "miles away", "separate from"   (no shared points)
  OVERLAPS  — "partly in", "extends into", "straddles"     (partial interior overlap)
  EQUALS    — "identical perimeter", "same boundary"      (exact same geometry)

WARNING — ambiguous vernacular (read carefully):
  "on"  → could be touches (shared boundary) or crosses (river traverses city), not within/contains
  "county seat of"  → NOT within (city may extend into adjacent counties) → overlaps
  "suburb of"       → NOT touches (may be disjoint) → disjoint
  "surrounded by"   → may be touches (partial encirclement), not within
  "along" + river/road → likely crosses (full LineString traversal), not just touches
  "same as" / "mirrors" → may be equals only if BOTH geometries are truly identical extents

Rules:
1. DIRECTION CHECK (mandatory before answering):
   - "contains" means A encloses B; "within" means A is inside B.
   - Explicitly ask: does this predicate apply A→B or B→A?
   - If the data shows B encloses A, the answer is "within", NOT "contains".

2. TEXTUAL QUALIFIER PRIORITY (override geometric signals when contradictory):
   - "most sides", "surrounded by" (partial encirclement) → touches
   - "partly in", "extends into", "part of population in" → overlaps
   - "enclave of" → touches (shared boundary, not interior)
   - "across from" over water → touches (water boundary is shared in GIS)
   - "county seat of" alone does NOT imply within — the city may extend into adjacent counties → overlaps
   - "on" (river) ambiguous: could be touches (shared edge) or crosses (river traverses city). Use OSM evidence.

3. GEOMETRY TYPE CONSTRAINTS:
   - LineString (river, road) traversing a Polygon interior → crosses.
   - LineString touching only Polygon boundary (no interior overlap) → touches.
   - "At the mouth of" / "upstream from" → check bbox and polygon; often crosses or disjoint.
   - Two Polygons partially sharing area → overlaps.

4. BOUNDING BOX CAUTION (never conclude directly from bbox):
   - bbox overlap does NOT imply polygon overlap.
   - bbox containment ≠ within/contains. Use polygon geometry as primary evidence.
   - Treat bbox as a clue, never as proof.

5. BBOX GAP TOLERANCE:
   - A bbox gap of < 0.01 degrees (~1 km) is NOT definitive proof of disjoint.
   - Administrative boundaries often share exact edges with 0-meter gap.

6. ENTITY VALIDATION:
   - Verify the OSM entity matches the expected state/region from the text.
   - If the entity location does not match the context, treat its geometry as unreliable.

7. LINESTRING (river) REASONING (use the entire geometry, not just centroid):
   - A river centroid may fall outside a city even if the river traverses it.
   - For "on" with a city (Polygon) and a river (LineString), consider both touches (shared boundary) and crosses (river traverses city interior).

8. PICK EXACTLY ONE predicate from: contains, within, touches, crosses, disjoint, overlaps, equals.

9. End with: Answer: [predicate]"

  [ToT] ✅ FINAL PREDICTION: equals

Does the vernacular "across from" imply a directional relationship (A→B) or a non-directional one (B→A)? 
If the text suggests A is inside B, the answer is "within" (not "contains").
If the text suggests B is inside A, the answer is "within" (not "contains").
If the vernacular is ambiguous, explore geometric possibilities (bbox, centroid, polygon). 
Use the 4-step disambiguation below (run before concluding within, touches, or overlaps).

--- 4-STEP DISAMBIGUATION (use the OSM polygon geometry, not just the centroid) ---
1. Do A and B have the EXACT SAME geometry (identical extent)? -> equals
2. Is A completely inside B? -> within (not overlaps)
3. Is B completely inside A? -> contains (not overlaps)
4. Do A and B share SOME area but NEITHER fully contains the other? -> overlaps

We are an expert in geospatial topological reasoning.

Vernacular-to-Topology Reference:
  WITHIN    — "is in", "located in", "part of"              (A is fully inside B)
  CONTAINS  — "is home to", "includes", "hosts"             (A fully encloses B)
  TOUCHES   — "borders", "adjacent to", "across from" (water boundary)  (partial, no interior intersection)
  CROSSES   — "passes through", "along", "traverses",
              "at the mouth of" / "upstream from" (full LineString)
  DISJOINT  — "far from", "miles away", "separate from" (large distance)
  OVERLAPS  — "partly in", "extends into", "straddles",
              "population in", "county seat of" (may extend into adjacent counties)
  EQUALS    — "identical perimeter", "same boundary as", "mirrors exactly",
              "exact same extent as", "perfect mapping symmetry"
              (A and B share the exact same geometry: same interior, boundary, exterior)

WARNING — ambiguous vernacular (read carefully):
  "county seat of" → does NOT imply within — the city may extend into adjacent counties → overlaps
  "suburb of"      → does NOT imply touches — the suburb may be separated from the main city -> disjoint
  "surrounded by"  → may be touches (partial encirclement), not within (full containment)
  "enclave of"     → touches (shared boundary, not interior)
  "along" + river/road → likely crosses (full LineString traversal), not just touches
  "same as" / "mirrors" -> may be equals only if BOTH geometries are truly identical extents

Rules:
1. DIRECTION CHECK (mandatory before answering):
   — "contains" means A encloses B; "within" means A is inside B.
   — Explicitly ask: does this predicate apply A→B or B→A?
   — If the data shows B encloses A, the answer is "within", NOT "contains".

2. TEXTUAL QUALIFIER PRIORITY (override geometric signals when contradictory):
   — "most sides", "surrounded by" with incomplete encirclement -> touches.
   — "partly in", "extends into", "part of population in" -> overlaps.
   — "enclave of" -> touches (shared boundary, not interior).
   — "across from" over water -> touches (water boundary is shared in GIS).
   — "county seat of" alone does NOT imply within.
   — "suburb of" alone does NOT imply touches — may be disjoint.

3. GEOMETRY TYPE CONSTRAINTS:
   — LineString (road, highway) traversing a Polygon interior -> crosses.
   — LineString touching only Polygon boundary -> touches.
   — "At the mouth of" / "upstream from" -> check bbox; may be crosses if LineString fully traverses polygon.
   — Two Polygons partially sharing area -> overlaps.

4. BOUNDING BOX CAUTION (never conclude directly from bbox):
   — bbox overlap ≠ polygon overlap. Polygons inside overlapping bboxes may still be disjoint.
   — bbox containment ≠ within/contains. A country bbox contains almost all nearby entities.
   — Treat bbox as a clue, not proof.

5. BBOX GAP TOLERANCE:
   — A bbox gap of < 0.01 degrees (~1 km) is NOT definitive evidence of disjoint.
   — Administrative boundaries often share exact edges with 0-meter gap.

6. ENTITY VALIDATION:
   — Verify the OSM entity matches the expected state/region.
   — If the entity location does not match the context, treat its geometry as unreliable.

7. VERBALLY EXPLAIN your reasoning using the OSM data above, NOT just the bbox. Do not guess.

8. Pick EXACTLY ONE predicate from: contains, within, touches, crosses, disjoint, overlaps, equals.

9. End with: Answer: [predicate]"
